 A TES OF A

ABSTRACT
MCNP was used to assess the neutronic impacts of absent elements in the Annular Core Research
Reactor (ACRR). Removing a single element increases the maximum peaking factor from its
nominal ≈1.5 to ⪆2. With a free-field cavity, this peaking occurs in the second row of fuel adjacent
to raised transient rods. A positive-reactivity object in the cavity—such as the polyethylene-
lead-graphite bucket (PLG-1)—raises peaking slightly further (to ≈2.1), typically in first-row
fuel. Neighboring elements also have elevating peaking factors (⪆1.75 in up to six elements).
A missing tri-element leads to maximum peaking of ≈2.35. Intra-element peaking, normally
≈1.3 and azimuthally symmetric, exceeds 2.0 and skews toward the missing location. Auxiliary
studies examine neutronic effects of various system configurations (rod positions, buckets, and
external cavities), model pedigree compared to measurements, and ACRR reflector neutronic design
facets.

SUMMARY
Neutronic effects of ACRR operating with missing elements are analyzed using MCNP. Results are
focused on assessing power distributions with a single missing element. Missing elements include
standard fuel elements, the 90% fuel elements, instrumented fuel elements, and nickel reflector
elements. The purpose and scope of this work are provided in Section 1. Modeling assumptions,
techniques, and analytical tools are discussed in Section 2.
As discussed in Section 3.2, a missing element can increase maximum peaking from ≈1.5 to ≈2.
With a free-field central cavity, this peaking generally occurs in the second row of fuel adjacent
to raised transient rods. With a positive-reactivity object in the cavity (e.g., PLG-1), maximum
peaking is ≈2.1 and occurs in first-row fuel elements (see Appendix A). For a missing inner-core
element, up to six surrounding elements exhibit elevated peaking factors of ⪆1.75.
In addition to greater fuel-element-averaged power peaking, missing elements create steep intra-
element power gradients in adjacent fuel. Refer to Section 3.3.1 and Section 3.3.2. Nominal
intra-element peaking is ≈1.3 (i.e., the edge of the fuel produces 1.3 times more power than the
overall average) and is generally symmetric. With a missing element, intra-element peaking can
exceed 2.0 and is asymmetrically tilted towards the missing element.
Reactivity changes associated with a single missing element are generally small but warrant detailed
evaluation. These reactivity effects are presented in Section 3.2.2. Excluding the innermost row, the
absence of inner-core elements produces a positive reactivity (maximum worth of ≈25¢) addition
due to the under-moderated design of the ACRR fuel lattice. The removal of first-row fuel decreases
reactivity by 24¢–29¢ per element.
Also analyzed is the tri-element facility where all three elements are assumed to have been removed
concurrent with reactor operation (see Section 3.4). Maximum peaking with a missing tri-element
is ≈2.35, and the associated intra-element peaking exceeds 2.2.
Auxiliary analyses in appendices include the power-peaking impacts of regulating rod positions
and standard buckets. Calculated reactor neutronic and cavity radiation metrics are compared to
measured values to establish model pedigree in Appendix B. Appendix C reviews the neutronic
effects of the nickel reflector elements. Alternative reflector arrangements are considered. The
nickel primarily acts to suppress fission peaking in outer fuel elements due to thermalized neutrons
returning from the pool water, which decreases reactivity by ≈$3 (i.e., the reactivity change between
complete nickel vs. water reflection) and reduces the prompt neutron generation time (≈24 µs vs.
≈30 µs with water reflection).

 11

 12

1 INTRODUCTION
The ACRR fuel lattice is designed to minimize operational variations in power peaking [1, 2, 3].
A tight pitch, a uniform fuel loading, and fuel-followed regulating rods constitute this nuclear
design feature. Missing elements in the lattice create neutronic discontinuities inconsistent with
the documented reactor design. Thus, neutronic analysis of missing elements is warranted to inform
reactor safety, fuel health, and thermal-mechanical performance.

1.1 Purpose
The neutronic impacts of the ACRR operating with a missing core element are investigated. Routine
operations such as fuel inspections and reactor characterization efforts (assessing reactivity worths
or moving instrumented elements) entail the removal and transfer of fuel elements. There could also
be experimental justification—such as placement of a specimen in the fuel lattice for irradiation
(e.g., in the tri-element facility)—for reactor operation with absent elements. Hypothetically, the
reactor could be mistakenly operated without replacement of a removed element. Neutronic metrics
of such conditions are documented to provide information for separate, transient thermal-hydraulic
analyses. These results may be referenced by the ACRR Documented Safety Analysis (DSA) and
other efforts supporting safety, fuel health/performance, and future design modifications.
Given the under-moderated design of the ACRR fuel lattice, removal of a single element will tend to
increase local peaking in adjacent fuel. The primary results are therefore maximum peaking factors
(PF) for each analyzed condition. These PFs may be used in subsequent reactor thermal-hydraulic
analyses to ascertain the consequences of the missing element. Reactivity changes and power
distributions are evaluated as are localized flux changes (for select cases) in the region surrounding
the missing element.

1.2 Scope
The scope of the analyses is limited to ACRR standalone operation with its nickel plate inserted.
The FREC-II is assumed to be decoupled and tilted away with its B4 C FREC rods lowered (poison
inserted). Thus, the effects of the nearby FREC-II assembly are effectively neglected for the missing-
element calculations. Auxiliary analyses of FREC-II in coupled and decoupled states are provided
in Appendix C for a reflector sensitivity study. Except for the tri-element facility and the reflector
study, the main analyses in Section 3 focus on analyzing a single missing element at a time.
Considered missing elements include standard fuel elements, 90%1.1 fuel elements, instrumented1.2
fuel elements, and nickel reflector elements. Missing regulating rods are not considered. Figure 1.2
shows a representative core map of element types as they typically exist throughout the ACRR
grid locations. The generic positions of fuel elements and regulating rods (comprised of the control
rod (CR) bank, transient rod (TR) bank, and safety rod (SR) bank) do not change frequently. The
generic positions of the nickel reflector elements also do not frequently change [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6].
The light blue "HE" hollow elements in Fig. 1.2 are treated as hollow aluminum tubes, which
are used in MCNP modeling of ACRR to represent various structures that displace water near
the lattice (e.g., self-powered neutron and plant-protection detectors). As modeled, most hollow

1.1
 These fuel elements are referred to as "90% elements" since each contains about 90% of the nominal fuel load (101
 g 235 U) of a standard fuel element. 90% elements are generally located at outer core locations and are comprised
 of smaller diameter UO2 -BeO pellets.
1.2
 Besides potential user tracking and analysis of the specific element, instrumented fuel elements in the MCNP
 models are identical to standard fuel elements.

 13

elements are outside the nickel reflector layer, excluding four elements at the neutron radiography
system (NRS) interface. The position and number of elements outside the nickel reflector layer are
of minimal consequence for gross core neutronics. The four aluminum elements on the north side
of the core (towards the NRS) are inside the nickel layer and thus could entail greater neutronic
effects (e.g., if they were hollow vs. solid vs. water-filled). These four elements displace excess
water and minimize neutron attenuation between the ACRR and NRS. Sandia MCNP models of
ACRR have consistently (i.e., for decades [5, 6, 7, 8, 9]) treated the four elements at the NRS
interface as hollow void or gas-filled aluminum tubes (see ACPR drawing P36583 [10]); analyses
here likewise treat the interface elements as hollow aluminum tubes.
Figure 1.1 depicts 2 of the 4 aluminum NRS interface elements. Given their longer end fittings and
the lack of visible threaded top caps, these elements are not ACPR hollow aluminum elements per
P36583 [10]. Hence, these interface elements may actually be solid aluminum rather than hollow
tubes. However, given their position and aluminum’s neutronic properties, the treatment of these
elements should not meaningfully change the conclusions of this report.

  Figure 1.1: Close-up view of (2 of 4) aluminum elements on north side of ACRR (near NRS nickel-aluminum
 window)

Neutronic effects of the following (individually) absent elements are evaluated:
  • Each standard fuel-element location identified in red as "FE" in Fig. 1.2
  • The tri-element location signified by the brownish "TE" element in Fig. 1.2, which are locations
    328, 329, and 433 [3]
  • The 90% elements denoted by the light-red/coral "EE" elements in Fig. 1.2, which are typically
    located at 705, 706, 707, 718, 725, 726, 727, 745, 746, and 747
  • Both of the instrumented fuel-element locations (101 and 218) identified in orange as "IE" in
 Fig. 1.2
  • Each of the nickel reflector-element locations identified in grey as "NE" in Fig. 1.2

 Figure 1.2: ACRR Core Map with Grid Number Locations

The three fuel elements comprising the tri-element facility have their end fittings mechanically
coupled, enabling simultaneous handling. Hence, removal of the tri-element is a unique case
considered. Figure 1.3 is a photograph of the tri-element coupling above the top grid plate. Upon
removal of these three rods, water fills the empty positions in the lattice and grid plates.

 15

 Figure 1.3: Upper fixture for tri-element facility

Principal neutronic metrics are evaluated with the reactor near delayed critical (DC). Peaking
factors and reactivity changes are assessed with the CR bank positioned near DC. For select cases,
calculations are conducted with the CRs very close to DC using rod-searching automation scripts.
Nevertheless, the results demonstrate that key PF metrics (e.g., maximum PF) are not very sensitive
to CR position. Follow-on work may expand the scope to consider the impacts to both pulse (e.g.,
with CRs near $3.5 and added analysis of neutron kinetic properties) and transient rod withdrawal
(TRW) operations. For pulse operations, PFs are quantified with the CRs positioned to set the
pulse size and the TRs (and SRs) fully raised, since ACRR pulses initiate after the TRs are fully
raised or nearly so. For TRW operations, significant power generation can occur with the TRs at an
intermediate position—with the SRs raised and the CRs at an intermediate position—depending
on the transient. Strictly speaking, the presented PFs are most applicable to steady operation and
to slow transients where the CRs are set within ≈0.5$ of DC.

 16

2 MODELING APPROACH AND ASSUMPTIONS
The missing element configurations are analyzed individually using a series of MCNP (version 6.3.1)
[11] calculations. Results are derived from execution of MCNP in KCODE mode. ENDF/B-VIII.0
cross sections are generally used. When key material isotopic data lacks gamma-ray-production
cross sections (e.g., cadmium), older elemental data is used instead. The following subsections
further describe the tools, input models, and assumptions.

2.1 Codes and Tools
A summary of the codes, data, and tools used in this work is as follows:
 • MCNP6.3 was built with hybrid parallelism for efficient execution on Sandia high performance
 computing (HPC) platforms.
 • MCNP utility tools are used to automate creation of inputs, execution of jobs, and post-
 processing of outputs.2.1
 • Mesh tally figures are generated directly from MCNP runtape files using the NOTEK command
 line option and external, python-based tools. Postscript files generated by MCNP are converted
 to PDF using Ghostscript.
 – MCNP plot command example using NOTEK:
 mcnp6 z notek run=RUNTPE com=PLOTCOMMANDFILE plotm=OUTFILE
 – MCNP-generated figures have the benefit of superimposing the mesh tally results over
 geometry surfaces.
A thorough record and description of the tools, input decks, and output files for this work can be
accessed through the Sandia TA-V Aras Innovator instance [13].2.2

2.2 MCNP Input Models
MCNP input models and supporting scripts are tracked on Sandia’s internal GitLab.2.3 Portions
of the repository are dedicated to ACRR modeling. These ACRR models are also tracked on
a separate repository, which includes added ACRRF-specific features (e.g., facility model, tilting
FREC-II, full NRS tube).2.4
MCNP models used in this work are adapted from characterizations efforts of ACRR [5, 6, 7,
8, 9]. The pedigrees of these models have been analyzed in relationship to characterized cavity
radiation metrics [14, 15, 16, 17]. Models have also been compared to operationally-observed
ACRR neutronics, such as the DC positions of regulating rods for various configurations [5, 18, 19].
To substantiate model pedigree for this work, fundamental radiation and neutronic metrics are
presented in Appendix B.
Using the model-building tools and MCNP inputs from the GitLab repositories, a basic MCNP
ACRR input file is generated. For the individual missing element calculations, a standalone, free-
field cavity (with 32” pedestal) ACRR model is used with the default “low-fidelity” geometric
definitions of the core elements and radiography system. This MCNP representation of the ACRR
is consistent with Sandia characterization work [5, 6, 7, 8, 9]. A higher-fidelity representation of

 17

the ACRR is also available, which has increased resolution of the ACRR fuel pellets, niobium cups,
and core element end fittings. The high-fidelity model can affect reactivity predictions by ⪅10¢
(and generally decreases reactivity) [20, 21], and the impact on cavity radiation metrics is quite
small. Given the small impact on key results and the considerably longer CPU time (due to the
added surfaces) associated with the high-fidelity model option, the default low fidelity option is
used. Further details on executing the model editor can be found under the GitLab repositories.
The basic ACRR input file is manually edited to facilitate automated replacement and modification
of the lattice input. Superimposed mesh tallies—such as local neutron flux around the missing
element—are defined for select cases. Six additional hollow aluminum elements are incorporated
outside the nickel reflection layer to approximate the presence of neutron detectors and other
instrumentation [3, 5]. However, non-fuel structures outside the nickel reflection layer are generally
of minimal importance.
The main template MCNP file is modified by a Bash script to direct each case’s input file to the
correct lattice input (via a “READ FILE=” MCNP input record). A compressed folder containing
copies of input files and supporting scripts can be acquired through Aras [13].
Figure 2.1 shows MCNP geometry plots of the nominal ACRR configuration (with all elements)
juxtaposed with a missing-element case. The missing element is an instrumented fuel element at
position 218 next to TR-C. For the purposes of illustrating the core element layout, all regulating
rods are depicted in the lowered position.

(a) Default ACRR 236-element pulse configuration: all (b) Modified configuration with missing instrumented
 elements in nominal positions element at position 218 (left of TR-C)

 Figure 2.1: ACRR MCNP model configurations

2.3 Software Quality, Verification, and Validation
MCNP is developed by Los Alamos National Laboratory. It is government off-the-shelf software
and is listed on the Sandia safety software inventory database [22]. MCNP is developed with
modern version control (e.g., Git-based), verification and validation techniques (V&V), and formal

 18

bug reporting/resolution (through the MCNP discourse forum [23] or via mcnp_help@lanl.gov),
as documented by the developers [11, 24]. TA-V at Sandia has developed a Safety Software Quality
Assurance Plan (SSQAP) for MCNP6.3 [25] and previous versions of the code. The SSQAP
documents for MCNP6.3 provide a record of example V&V results and its acceptance for TA-V
applications (including safety). Builds of MCNP6.3.1 on the HPCs (under /projects/mcnp/mcnp631
and /projects/mcnp-fast) were compiled and tested following the developer’s instructions.
The calculations and analyses presented in this report adhere to the TA-V calculation procedure
TAV–AP–025 [26].

2.4 Assumptions
A bulleted summary of important assumptions and treatments for the main results (in Section 3)
is as follows:
 • PFs and reactivities are evaluated using the standalone ACRR MCNP model (with default
 simpler geometry) with the nickel plate in place. The nearby FREC-II assembly is neglected.
 – The algorithm for calculating PFs may be found in existing ACRR calculations (e.g.,
             ACRR-CALC-2023-003 [21]) and pertinent SAND reports (e.g., SAND2023-12117 [15]).
 – The central cavity is free-field with the 32” pedestal.2.5
 • Material densities and cross sections reflect room temperature (293 K) conditions.
 • PFs are calculated with the CRs at 1400 rod units (RU) (1 RU = 0.1 mm = 0.01 cm) for the
 base case (all fuel elements at their default positions) and for each missing element scenario.
 • For select cases, the CRs are adjusted using a rod-searching script to yield reactivity within
        3–5 cents (¢) of DC.
 – Adjoint-weighted βeff values calculated by MCNP are used to evaluate reactivity.
 • The TRs and SRs are fully raised.
 • Fuel elements of the tri-element facility can be removed individually, which assumes the
 upper coupling fixture can be removed. Ostensibly, the tri-element could also be removed
 and replaced with three independent standard fuel elements.
 – Removal of all three elements comprising tri-element is also considered as a unique case.
 • Axial peaking effects are neglected. PF values represent axially-integrated quantities over the
 active fuel length. Mesh tallies of fission heating and neutron flux are approximations of core
 mid-plane values. The mesh tallies are integrated over axial zones (with lengths of 5 cm to
 26 cm) to improve tally statistics.

2.5
 Alternative configurations with standard buckets (Appendix A), the 32” + 8” pedestals (Appendix B), and FREC-II
 (Appendix C) are assessed in the Appendices.

 19

 20

3 ANALYSIS
Table 3.1 lists the cases analyzed for the main analyses. Comparisons are drawn to the base case
(standalone ACRR with all elements per Fig. 1.2 and Fig. 2.1a) for each of the missing elements. The
base case is executed with CRs at 1400 RU and at DC (via rod searching). The main exploratory
analysis consists of removing (one element per calculation) 216 standard fuel elements, 10 90% fuel
elements, 2 instrumented fuel elements, and 50 nickel elements. Each of these cases are executed
with the CRs at 1400 RU to facilitate comparison to the base case. The most-limiting case from the
exploratory analysis, which is a missing instrumented element at position 218, is rod-searched to DC
and compared to the base case at DC. Finally, the tri-element is rod-searched to DC for comparison
to the base case. As explicated in the subsections below, tri-element removal exacerbates peaking
more than the removal of any single element, as expected.

 Table 3.1: Base and missing-element cases

 Set # Set description # cases CR position (RU)
 0 Base standalone ACRR 1 1400
 1 Rod-searched base ACRR near DC 1 1188
 2 Standard fuel elements 216 1400
          3                      90% fuel elements                           10           1400
 4 Instrumented fuel elements 2 1400
 5 Nickel reflector elements 50 1400
 6 Worst-case single missing fuel location (at 218) 1 1163
 7 Tri-element missing 1 1300
 Total cases 282

3.1 Base Case for Comparison
Figure 3.1 shows PFs for the base ACRR model executed at 1400 RU (ρ ≈ 0.5$) and 1188 RU
(near DC). The uncertainty in each rod’s PF is tracked in the analyses and is on the order of
1% or less—uncertainty values are not depicted in Fig. 3.1 (or any other power distribution in
this document) to improve the readability of the figure. The reactivity values above each of the
core diagrams include the Monte Carlo uncertainties: the parenthetical values denote one standard
deviation uncertainty (i.e., ±1σ) associated with the preceding digit.3.1 The largest PF impact due
to small (⪅500 RU) changes in CR position is the PF of the CRs themselves, which have UO2 -BeO
fuel followers. The CR PFs are generally not of critical importance for reactor operation.

The maximum PF for the base standalone ACRR is roughly 1.5 for both CR positions, as summarized
in Table 3.2. Position 219 (lower-left of TR-C) exhibits maximum peaking in both cases. The PFs
for inner fuel elements (those closer to the cavity) and adjacent to the other TRs are within 1% of
the maximum PF, indicative of the symmetric nature of the core design. The relative difference in
maximum PF between the 1400 RU base case and the rod-searched base case (CRs at 1188 RU) is
only 0.23%.

 Table 3.2: Base case ACRR PF metrics

 Base case with CRs rod-searched to DC (1188 RU) 1.5004(7) 219
 Percent difference 0.23(8)

The overall standalone ACRR power distribution and maximum PF are rather independent of
the CR and TR positions. Maximum PF varies by roughly 5%–10% depending on CR and TR
positions, as presented in Appendix A.

3.2 Absent Element Results Overview
This section provides results for the missing (standard) fuel, 90% fuel, instrumented fuel, and nickel
reflector elements. Each of these cases has the CRs set at 1400 RU to facilitate reactivity worth
estimates of each missing element.

Figure 3.2 is a core mapping of maximum PF resulting from each of the missing elements. Fig. 3.2
is not a power distribution and does not include the tri-element results, which are reserved for

Section 3.4. Uncertainties for the maximum PFs are ⪅1% and are excluded from the mapping plot

example:
  • The instrumented element at 218 is actually missing from the MCNP model, despite a circle
 being present at position 218 in Fig. 3.2.
  • Maximum PF naturally occurs at a different location other than the missing-element location.
 – For a missing element at 218, maximum PF occurs at position 219 (to the lower-right of
 218 and lower-left of TR-C) with a value ≈2.0. Refer to the focused analysis of location
         218 in Section 3.3.

 1.48
 Figure 3.2: Map of max. PFs for each missing element

For inner missing elements located closer to the cavity, maximum PF occurs at a fueled location
adjacent to the missing element. The gross core power distribution is peaked towards the inner
core (see Fig. 3.1), and the under-moderated lattice design entails increased local thermalization
near the missing element. These global and local effects compound to yield maximum PFs greater
than the base case value of ≈1.5. Fig. 3.2 shows that inner missing fuel elements entail maximum

 23

PFs ranging from 1.7 to 2.0. The worst-case missing fuel elements are generally adjacent to the
TRs—greater peaking is also observed near these elements in the default ACRR configuration.3.2
The outermost missing element results in Fig. 3.2 are for missing nickel elements. Any single
missing nickel element around the core has no significant impact on maximum PF. As discussed
in Appendix C, the removal of all nickel reflector elements (effectively creating a water-reflected
ACRR) causes significant changes in core neutronics.
For outer missing fuel elements (those located 4 or 5 rows from the cavity), there is still increased
local peaking near the missing element. However, the gross power distribution remains center
peaked: a single missing outer element does not significantly perturb the global power distribution.
Thus, removing outer elements leaves maximum PF close to the nominal value of 1.5, but the
location of maximum PF typically changes. Figure 3.3 shows PFs for an absent fuel element at
position 515, an outer core location in the fifth hexagonal row; this power distribution is juxtaposed
with the base case. In addition to increased local peaking around 515, reactivity increases by ≈7¢
over the base case.

Figure 3.4 translates the maximum PF information from Fig. 3.2 into a one-dimensional plot. Here,
maximum PF is plotted as a function of the radial position of the missing element (not the radial
location of maximum peaking). Error bars in this figure denote ±1σ. Fig. 3.4 further illustrates
how the removal of a single element beyond the fourth row, which is ≥30 cm from the cavity center,
results in maximum PF very similar to the base case.

3.2.2 Reactivity Changes
Estimated reactivity worths of each missing fuel and nickel element are provided by Figure 3.5.
The worth of the tri-element is not depicted (see Section 3.4). Here, worth is simply the reactivity
difference between the base case and the individual missing-element cases. Worth uncertainties in
Fig. 3.5 are ≈1 cent and are provided in Appendix D of the appendices.
Figure 3.6 shows alternative views of the information from Fig. 3.5: Fig. 3.6a focuses on missing
fuel-element worths and has the nickel elements denoted by the grey Ni circles, while Fig. 3.6b
focuses on the nickel worths. Excluding the first row, the removal of inner fuel elements increases
reactivity up to 25¢ (again due to the under-moderated lattice design), while the removal of outer
fuel or those adjacent to CRs tends to decrease reactivity by a small amount (–5¢ to –10¢). The
greatest reactivity increase at location 328, which also comprises the tri-element.3.3

3.3
 For core symmetry, location 328 could hypothetically field a third SR. The original offset loading tube, which has
 been removed, prevented placement of a SR at 328.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            0.288                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 0.0730
 (a) Focus on missing fuel elements (b) Focus on missing nickel reflector elements

 Figure 3.6: Reactivity worths of missing fuel (a) vs. nickel (b) elements

Innermost fuel elements have the greatest magnitude of worth (|∆ρ|). Despite the under-moderated
design of ACRR, the removal of first-row fuel decreases reactivity. The first row elements have

 26

worths of –25¢ to –29¢. The significant decrease in reactivity due to removal the first-row fuel is
corroborated by the DSA and historical ACRR neutronic studies [3, 27]. Furthermore, preconceptual
design efforts for the Combined Radiation Environments for Survivability Testing (CREST) facility
determined that the complete removal of the first fuel row decreased reactivity by –$11 total (which
would prevent critical operations), barring other compensatory design changes [14]. The negative
reactivity change due to removal of first-row fuel makes physical sense: fuel is being removed from
the high-flux, inner-core region, and this negative reactivity effect is not sufficiently counteracted
by increased local thermal flux. In contrast to fuel that is 1-2 rows deeper into the lattice, the first
row is adjacent to the dry steel cavity. The corner elements of the first fuel row (positions 101, 105,
109, 113, 117, and 121) have relatively small and negative worths of –4¢ to –7¢.
The nickel reflector elements have minimal individual worth—average nickel element worth is
roughly zero when considering stochastic error.3.4 There are a few exceptions to this trend, such as
the nickel elements at positions 621, 639, and 743; reflector elements at these positions seem to have
statistically significant worths that may be further investigated in future work. The uncertainty in
adjoint-weighted βeff , which is used to normalize keff differences, should also be considered when
assessing the significance of small reactivity changes.
Appendix D in the appendices provides a complete summary of reactivity and PF metrics for each
missing-element case with CRs set at 1400 RU.

3.3 Absent Element at 218
When considering a single missing element, the exploratory study from Section 3.2 indicated
increased power peaking due to removal of fuel adjacent to the TRs. In particular, missing fuel
elements facing the cavity and adjacent to a TR lead to higher PFs. This section focuses on a
missing fuel element at 218, which is to the left of TR-C.
Fuel at position 219, which is immediately below 218 and TR-C, consistently appears to exhibit
the greatest PF in ACRR MCNP models with a free-field cavity and CRs positioned near DC. As
modeled, the presence of the neutron radiography system and unique features associated with the
interface (aluminum elements and nickel plate) on the TR-C (north) side of the reactor are likely
explanations for the slight tilt in power towards TR-C and location 219. Inclusion of the decoupled
and tilted FREC-II—which is neglected in this section—could subtly affect power tilting in the
core.
Other missing elements near the TRs have very similar peaking compared to a missing element at
218. These elements include those at positions 228, 229, 333, 335 around TR-A, positions 208, 209,
309, 311 around TR-B, and positions 219, 321, 323 around TR-C. Hence, such peaking behavior
(i.e., maximum PF ≈2.0) is not limited to an instrumented element next to TR-C. While the main
results entail a free-field central cavity with the 32” pedestal, peaking with standard buckets in the
cavity is provided in Appendix A.
Figure 3.7 shows PF distributions of the base case compared to a missing element at 218. These
results are derived via rod-searching both the base case and the missing-element case to within 3¢ of
DC. The rod-searched PFs for a missing element at 218 are very similar to the results with CRs at
1400 RU (i.e., from Fig. 3.2). Compared to the results at 1400 RU, the rod-searched maximum PF
has increased just ≈1% to 1.99 (from 1.98), which is within the 1% relative errors due to stochastic
uncertainty. Maximum PF occurs at the same location in both cases, which is location 219. With

3.4
 Removal of all nickel reflection has significant neutronic changes, as discussed in Appendix C.

 27

a missing fuel element at 218, the DC position of the CRs is 1163 RU, for a difference of 25 RU (2.5
mm) compared to the base case (1188 RU for DC). With the CRs fixed at 1400 RU, the removal
of the element at 218 increases reactivity by 10¢.

3.3.1 Local Mesh Tally Analysis for Absent 218
Figure 3.8 compares specific fission heating surrounding location 218 for the base case and the
missing element (at 218) case. Relative to the base case, peak fission heating is greater for the
missing-element case. Peak fission heating in Fig. 3.8b is roughly 50-60% greater than the base
case. This trend is expected given the difference in maximum PF between the cases (≈2.0 for the
missing-element case vs. ≈1.5 for the base case) and intra-element power peaking. Nominal intra-
element peaking for ACRR is ≈1.3 based on Fig. 3.8a, consistent with documented core design
characteristics [1, 2, 3, 28]. The intra-element power gradients are significantly increased in the
missing-element case because of the large increase in local thermal flux impinging upon the outer
surfaces of surrounding fuel.

 Figure 3.8: Local fission heating around location 218 compared to base case

Figure 3.9 compares thermal3.5 neutron fluxes around position 218. Compared to the base case
(Fig. 3.9a), the significant increase (>2X) in local thermal flux due to the missing element (Fig. 3.9b)
is obvious. Being a thermal spectrum reactor, thermal flux dictates much of the PF distribution
behavior over the ACRR. Figure 3.10 illustrates the global distributions of thermal neutron fluxes
for these cases. Thermal flux is peaked around the TRs, at position 218 (for the absent element
case), throughout the coolant channels between fuel elements, and outside the nickel reflection
layer (particularly below the nickel plate, which in reality might be affected by the tilted FREC-II
assembly). Thermal flux is depressed in outer core regions due to neutron absorption in nickel
reflector elements. Appendix C provides neutronic analyses of ACRR reflector arrangements,
including the minor effects of external cavities.

3.3.2 Intra-element Heating Gradient for Absent 218
Intra-element heating gradients are examined for positions 217 and 219, which are both adjacent
to position 218. Position 217 is nominally surrounded by six fuel elements. Position 219 is adjacent
to a TR, and its other five neighbors are fuel elements. The intra-element heating gradients are
assessed with a missing element at position 218 and compared to the base case with no missing
elements. Figure 3.11 shows specific fission heating magnitudes around the missing 218 position.
Positions 217 and 219 are identified in Fig. 3.11.

 30

 Position 217
 Fission

 W/g per MW

 Fuel at 218 TR-C
 5 removed

 3

 Position 219
 Central
 cavity

 Figure 3.11: Local fission heating around missing 218 position (instrumented fuel element at 218 absent)

Figure 3.12 shows intra-element heating at position 217. This information is derived from a
cylindrical mesh tally with 0.1 mm radial zones and 3 degree azimuthal zones. The tally is
integrated axially over a 6 cm segment at the active fuel midplane. The x- and y-axes in Fig. 3.12
are consistent with the MCNP model coordinates: as depicted in this report, the x-axis corresponds
to the lateral (“left” and “right”) directions of the MCNP model, and the y-axis corresponds to
the vertical directions.3.6 Subtle statistical noise is (barely) visible due to the fine resolution of
the mesh. As shown in Figure 3.14, maximum relative error is ≈2%. Because position 217 is
nominally surrounded by fuel elements, Fig. 3.12a exhibits intra-element gradients consistent with
the ACRR nuclear design [2, 3]. Figure 3.13 shows the original, calculated intra-element power
profile for ACRR fuel [2]. There is impressive consistency between the legacy two-dimensional
discrete ordinates calculation, which utilized multi-group ENDF/B-III or -IV nuclear data, and
the modern Monte Carlo calculation performed with continuous-energy ENDF/B-III.0 data. Intra-
element peaking in most ACRR fuel elements (i.e., those not adjacent to regulating elements, the
cavity, or nickel structures) is ≈1.2–1.3. With a nominal fuel lattice, intra-element heating at 217
exhibits a slight tilt towards TR-C, even though it is not immediately adjacent to the TR, but the
profile is mostly symmetric. In contrast, with fuel absent at position 218, the intra-element heating
gradient at 217 is shifted towards the missing fuel and exceeds 2.0.

 Physically, the “right” (positive x) direction in the model is to the northeast.

3.4 Absent Tri-element
PFs for the missing tri-element are compared to the base case in Figure 3.16. With the tri-element

3.4.1 Local Mesh Tally Analysis for Absent Tri-element
Figure 3.17 shows fission heating around the tri-element location. This figure and the following
mesh tally plots compare the base case to the results with the missing tri-element. With three
missing co-located elements, the increased local thermal neutron flux is driving local fission heating
(especially at location 224) to be in excess of 13 W/g per MW (Fig. 3.17b). Peak intra-element
heating in the base ACRR is ≈5 W/g (per MW) for a typical fuel element (i.e., inner fuel that is not
adjacent to a regulating rod). In elements next to the missing tri-element, peak heating on the side
facing the (missing) tri-element is ≈15 W/g. Intra-element power gradients near missing elements
are also asymmetric compared to base-case intra-element heating. Shifted away from the missing
tri-element, inner fuel away heating is ≈5 W/g. Intra-element peaking is further examined in
Section 3.4.2. Further analysis can ascertain the thermal, hydraulic, and mechanical consequences
of such intra-element heating gradients.

 Figure 3.17: Local fission heating for absent tri-element compared to base case

Figure 3.18 shows local thermal neutron flux around the tri-element position. Figure 3.19 compares
local fast fluxes. Relative to the base ACRR, thermal neutron flux in the missing tri-element water
hole has increased over 4X to >2 × 1013 cm−2 s−1 per MW. In the base case, thermal fluxes in
the interstitial water channels around the tri-element position are ≈5 × 1012 cm−2 s−1 per MW.
Increased local thermal flux around the missing tri-element drives the associated increase in PFs,
particularly for the fuel element at location 224, and exacerbates intra-element heating profiles.
Due to its greater overall peaking factor, fast flux generated at 224 has also increased as shown by
Fig. 3.19b and Fig. 3.19a.

3.4.2 Intra-element Heating Gradient for Absent Tri-element
Intra-element heating profiles are further examined for position 224. With a nominal fuel lattice,
intra-element heating at 224 is symmetric and resembles 217’s peaking magnitude, as expected
(i.e., typical inner fuel has intra-element peaking of ≈1.3). In contrast, with the tri-element absent,
the intra-element heating gradient at 224 is shifted towards the missing fuel and exceeds 2.2. The
combination of high core peaking (PF ≈2.35) and high intra-element peaking at 224 makes the
missing tri-element the most challenging configuration identified.

3.5 Key PF Results Summary
Table 3.3 provides a summary of key results from this section. Most of these cases and their
neuronic characteristics have already been described. For a single missing element with a free-field
cavity, maximum PF is ≈2.0. For a missing tri-element, maximum PF is ≈2.35. Table 3.3 also lists
the number of fuel elements with PF exceeding 1.7, which signifies the degree of enhanced peaking
in the vicinity of the missing element.

4 CONCLUSIONS
The neutronic repercussions of ACRR operating with missing elements have been evaluated. Each
fuel and nickel element was individually removed and its absence was analyzed using MCNP. Fuel
element reactivity worths and core PFs were quantified. Operation with a missing tri-element
was also analyzed. No missing regulating elements were considered in this work. Missing element
calculations were conducted using MCNP models of the ACRR (derived from characterization
efforts) with the CRs positioned near critical and the SRs+TRs raised.
The following deductions were made from the analyses:
 • With a free-field cavity, a maximum PF of ≈2.0 is possible for a single missing fuel element,
 particularly if the element was near a TR.
 • With a free-field cavity, a maximum PF of ≈2.35 is possible for a missing tri-element.
 • For missing inner-core elements, 5–6 adjacent fuel elements experience increased PF (⪆1.75)
 in addition to greater intra-element power gradients (⪆2.0 vs. ≈1.3–1.5 nominal).
 • The reactivity worths for missing elements from the first row of fuel are significant and
 negative. These elements are worth –24¢ to –29¢ per element.
 • Missing inner fuel elements (excluding the first row) have positive individual worth up to 25¢.
 • Missing outer fuel and reflector elements have minimal individual worth and small impacts
 on maximum PF.
Select cases were further investigated using focused mesh tallies. Beyond increased element-
resolved PFs, sub-element tallies suggest that fuel elements adjacent to a missing element have
exacerbated intra-element power gradients. For a nominal core configuration, most fuel elements
exhibit azimuthally-symmetric intra-element peaking with a magnitude of ≈1.3. For the missing
element configurations, nearby fuel has asymmetric intra-element peaking with a magnitude of
⪆2.0. Thermal, hydraulic, and mechanical analyses of ACRR fuel elements may consider these
effects.
For future design studies and modifications, it is prudent to regularly evaluate intra-element power
gradients. The analyses presented here demonstrate that lattice discontinuities induce intra-element
power peaking inconsistent with the core’s intended design.
Supporting material is provided in the appendices. PFs as a function of CR and TR positions are
assessed in Appendix A. Standard buckets in the central cavity are also analyzed. Most important
for this work, it is established that maximum PF is not a very strong function of regulating
rod positions (for standalone ACRR operation). Model pedigree is discussed in Appendix B.
Appendix C presents a reflector study where the nickel reflector elements are removed, effectively
creating pure-water reflection. Various core configurations that include coupling with external
cavities are evaluated. Appendix D provides a complete listing of the main results for each missing-
element scenario.

APPENDIX A REGULATING ROD POSITIONS, BUCKETS, AND PEAKING
 FACTORS
Auxiliary analyses of maximum PFs as a function of rod positions (CRs and TRs) are presented here.
These analyses demonstrate that the maximum ACRR PF depends on it regulating rod positions.
However, maximum PF changes less than 10% when CR and TR positions are translated. These
calculations are presented for a free-field central cavity and for standard buckets, each case utilizing
the 32” pedestal.
According to MCNP predictions, maximum PF monotonically decreases as the CRs are raised as
shown in Figure A.1. In these calculations, the SRs and TRs are fully raised for all CR positions.
The maximum PF varies by ≈8% over the full range of CR positions. PF distributions for select
CR positions are provided in Figure A.2. With the TRs raised and a free-field cavity, maximum
peaking is consistently an inner fuel element adjacent to a TR position.

 Figure A.2: ACRR peaking factors for different CR positions, free-field

As a function of TR position, maximum PF varies by a smaller (≈4%) amount but exhibits more
complicated trends. In the calculations in Figure A.3, the CRs are positioned for a $3.5 pulse at
2350 RU, and the SRs are fully raised. No TR pedestals are assumed in the model, which would
limit the minimum TR position (4370 RU is typical [3]). As the TRs are raised, maximum PF is
initially ≈1.44 and is not sensitive to TR position. At 3000 RU to 6000 RU, maximum PF decreases
to ≈1.405. After 6000 RU, maximum PF increases to ≈1.46.

                                         0   1000   2000      3000    4000         5000          6000         7000          8000   9000
 ACRR TR bank position (rod units)
 Figure A.3: Max. PF vs. TR position (SRs raised, CRs set to 2350 RU for $3.5 pulse), free-field

Upon inspection of the PF distributions depicted in Figure A.4, the location of maximum PF is
perturbed by the raising of the TRs. With the TRs down (poison inserted into the active core
region), maximum PF occurs near the tri-element facility at location 224, as shown in Fig. A.4a.
The power distribution is rather static up to the 3500 RU (Fig. A.4b), where the system is near DC.
At 4500–5500 RU (Fig. A.4c), the peak element has subtly shifted towards TR-A, and maximum PF
occurs in the first row of fuel (primarily locations 104 and 121). The power shift toward TR-A may
result from the fuel allocation at the FREC-II interface. A nearly complete hexagonal flat exists on
the TR-A (eastern) side of ACRR, which improves coupled performance with FREC-II.A.1 With
the TRs at 6000 RU (Fig. A.4d), power shifts to the second fuel row closer to the TRs—at higher
TR positions, the bias towards TR-A has disappeared, and PFs are similar around the three TRs.
As the TRs are fully raised to 9000 RU (Fig. A.4e and Fig. A.4f), peaking further increases around
the TRs and reaches a maximum of ≈1.46 (with CRs at 2350 RU for a $3.5 pulse). Meaningful
pulse power in ACRR initiates once the TRs are fully raised (or nearly so) [29]. Hence, only the
PF distributions for higher TR positions are of practical significance for ACRR pulse neutronics.

A.1
 FREC-II is not represented (even in a decoupled state) in the particular MCNP models used in this section.

 Figure A.4: ACRR peaking factors for different TR positions (CRs set to 2350 RU), free-field

PF variation as a function of CR position are evaluated for standard ACRR buckets. LB-44,
CdPoly, LP-1, and PLG-1 are analyzed. LB-44, CdPoly, and LP-1 have negative reactivity worths
of roughly –$6, –$4, and –$3, respectively [3, 30, 31]. LB-44 contains boron poison, and CdPoly
has cadmium. Although LP-1 lacks a dedicated poison material, it entails a polyethylene layer 0.8”
in thickness, which contributes to its negative reactivity worth. Smaller quantities of polyethylene,
such as the 0.25” of polyethylene (in combination with 0.25” of graphite) in PLG-1, can entail
positive reactivity worth. PLG-1 is the only standard bucket with a positive reactivity worth,
which is about $0.75 [32].
PF results for LB-44 are provided in Figure A.5 and Figure A.6. Relative to free field, maximum
PFs for LB-44 are reduced in magnitude (particularly in the first row), are a weaker function of
CR position (varying about 3%), and are pushed out to the third row of fuel (but still near a
TR). Buckets with polyethylene cause increased peaking in the first row. PF results for CdPoly
are provided in Figure A.7 and Figure A.8. PF results for LP-1 are provided in Figure A.9 and
Figure A.10. PF results for PLG-1 are provided in Figure A.11 and Figure A.12. LP-1 and PLG-1,
having polyethylene and no overt poison materials, have increased peaking in the first row. LP-1 has
the greatest variation in PF as a function of CR position (about 10%), but PLG-1 has the greatest
PF when CRs are near DC. The positive reactivity worth of PLG-1 plays a role in this peaking
because it drives CR positions lower, pushing the power distribution inward and exacerbating inner
row peaking.

 Figure A.14: Reactivity metrics vs. CR position for free field and standard buckets

 Figure A.15 shows the relationship between maximum PF and TR position with the PLG-1 bucket
 in the central cavity. Unlike the free-field trend, maximum PF with PLG-1 only decreases as the

 57

TRs are raised. PF distributions for select TR positions are provided in Figure A.16. Because
the PLG-1 bucket entails the greatest PFs (when CRs are positioned near DC), it is prudent to
examine PFs with a missing element and the PLG-1 bucket in the cavity. Figure A.17 shows PF
with a missing element at core location 224. With the PLG-1 bucket and a nominal core layout,
maximum peaking occurs in the first row (typically at location 103, 119, or 120). Thus, a fuel
element at location 224 is removed, which is near locations 119 and 120, and PF distributions are
evaluated with CRs set at 900 RU (near DC with PLG-1 and the fuel element removed) and 2000
RU (representative of a large pulse). Consistent with the main results in this report, which are
with free-field cavities, maximum PF is ≈2 with a single missing element and the PLG-1 bucket in
the cavity.

APPENDIX B ACRR MCNP MODEL PEDIGREE
The pedigree of the ACRR MCNP model used in this work is established in relationship to
characterized cavity radiation metrics and observed DC rod positions. The model used was derived
from those utilized in major characterization efforts [6, 7, 8, 30, 32, 31]. Modifications have been
made over the years to enable model tractability on GitLab, making the model more flexible for
both ACRRF and CREST analysis.
Table B.1 provides reactor model metrics in comparison to observed and characterized values [5, 6].
The percentages in parentheses denote 1-σ relative errors. Radiation metrics in the free-field central
cavity are in good agreement. Using the standalone ACRR model, MCNP-calculated values are
within ≈4% of characterized values from [6].

 † Measured CR DC positions are operator-reported values spanning the 46 years of operations with the 236 element core configuration and
 a free-field central cavity (and no coupled FREC). The 236 element configuration was established around reactor run #453 on 1980-04-08
   [33, 34]. DC position ranges are inspired by reactor runs $453 (1980-04-08), #1903 (1984-01-09), #3447 (1988-01-06), #4502 (1990-01-04),
   #5770 (1994-02-08), #6669 (2000-01-03), #7936 (2004-01-06), #9405 (2010-01-28), #10163 (2012-01-05), #10785 (2014-02-04), and #12322
 (2018-01-25) [34, 35]. Facility reconfigurations over the years make pre-2000 DCs of less use, which was before the central cavity change
 and several other modifications, and so the table values chiefly reflect post-2000 values. Measured cavity radiation values are from published
 characterization efforts [6].

Predicted DC positions of the CR bank are in less agreement with operational values. Discrepancies
in observed vs. modeled CR DC position indicate a positive reactivity bias in the MCNP model
consistent with previous analytical efforts [5, 18, 36]. The reactivity bias is $0.5–$1 depending on
the reactor configuration and model options (e.g., material definitions and cross section libraries).
Fortunately, ACRR power peaking is not a significant function of exact regulating rod positions,
as observed in Appendix A and ACRR neutronic design descriptions [2, 3].
The DC positions in Table B.1 provide “representative” ranges of the actual ACRR DC positions,
which grossly capture the variability due to fission product buildup, temperature fluctuations, and
system configuration changes. Even for “identical” or similar system configurations, observed DC
positions may change for a variety of reasons. Over the decades of ACRRF operation, there has
been small fuel burnup (⪅1%) and many system configuration changes. Operator logs show that
DC positions can fluctuate by ≈10 RU (≈3¢) over day-to-day operations (ostensibly due to minor

 61

system fluctuations and different operators). In general, however, CR DC positions (SRs and
TRs raised) are ≈1500–1550 RU with a free-field ACRR central cavity, FREC decoupled, and the
32” pedestal. The addition of the 8” pedestal contributes a small and positive reactivity change,
lowering CR DC positions to 1410–1460 RU; hence, the observed worth of the 8” pedestal is ≈15–
30¢. Despite the MCNP model bias, the model also predicts a positive reactivity change due to the
8” pedestal of ≈27¢.

 62

APPENDIX C REMOVAL OF ALL NICKEL REFLECTOR ELEMENTS
This appendix provides supplementary results and insights into the neutronic effects of removing the
nickel components surrounding the ACRR. The question of reflector impacts on ACRR performance
frequently arises for ACRR configuration changes and future designs (such as for implementation
in the planned CREST facility).
For an epithermal system, the placement of nickel—a mild absorber of thermal- and intermediate-
energy neutrons—around the core is a curious design choice. The ACPR predecessor to ACRR did
not have nickel (or any other material) reflector elements as a normal feature. However, there are
neutronic design reasons for the placement of nickel reflectors around the ACRR. These neutronic
effects are reviewed and documented in this appendix.
Compared to reflection by the pool water, nickel reflection decreases reactivity of the ACRR.C.1
Thus, in the context of providing “reflector savings” for reactivity, the nickel elements do not
really function as a neutron reflector. In alternative configurations, nickel reflection could inversely
increase reactivity (e.g., relative to air/void, if the ACRR was not a pool reactor). However, as
nominally configured, nickel reflection in ACRR principally acts to:

 • Decrease excess reactivity relative to water reflection [2].
 – The nickel absorbs thermal- and intermediate-energy neutrons that would otherwise
 be returning from the pool water and causing additional fissions in the outer core. If
 the nickel is removed (with reactor pool water taking its place), there are increased
 fission reactions in the outer core due to thermalized neutrons, which increases reactivity.
 Replacing the nickel with alternative materials creates different neutronic effects, as
 elaborated in Appendix C.2.
 – The reactivity decrease is contingent on the nickel layer being a single row thick and
 consisting of elements with diameter similar to the fuel elements. Thicker nickel reflection
 produces unique neutronic effects.
 – Although reactivity is lowered at all rod positions, the absence of nickel elements (i.e.,
 water reflection) slightly decreases the bank worths of the CRs, TRs, and SRs. The
 decreased bank worth is tied to changes in the core power distribution: water reflection
 causes increased peaking in the outer core, and the shift in power from the inner to outer
 core lessens the worth of the regulating rods.
 • Push the global power distribution inward relative to water reflection [2].
 – Being a thermal and resonance absorber, the nickel absorbs lower-energy neutrons return-
 ing from the pool water.
 – Absorption of thermalized neutrons returning from the pool water suppresses edge power
 peaking in outer core fuel elements.
 – By pushing the power distribution inward, cavity radiation metrics (e.g., fluence per MJ)
 are increased. In contrast, pure-water reflection flattens the power distribution slightly,
 reducing per-MJ radiation metrics.
 • Decrease neutron generation time relative to water reflection [2, 3].
 – Without the nickel elements, pure-water reflection causes greater outer core power
 peaking (due to thermal neutron fissions), which increases overall neutron lifetime.
 Longer neutron lifetime deleteriously increases pulse width.

C.1
 Historically, the ACRR actually operated with complete water reflection (and other significant system changes)
 during the 1990s for a temporary venture into molybdenum isotope production [4].

 63

These basic notions are known and documented in historical ACRR design documents [1, 2, 37].
Still, in the spirit of evaluating ACRR neutronic changes due to missing elements using modern
tools (i.e., MCNP6 and ENDF/B-VIII data), the ACRR is analyzed with all its exterior nickel
structures removed.

C.1 Overall Neutronic Effects
Barring replacement with an alternative material, water naturally fills the space of the removed
nickel structures. In the MCNP model, the removed nickel includes the reflector elements, the
eastern FREC-II decoupling plate, and the northern NRS interface plate.
Removal of the nickel surrounding the ACRR increases reactivity by approximately $3 [2]. Table C.1
summarizes how reactivity and neutron generation time change between nickel and water reflection.
Eight configurations are presented. Figure C.1 and Figure C.2 illustrate the configurations. Each
scenario has the CRs fixed at 1202 RU (near DC for the base model in this report), SRs and TRs
fully raised, and the 32” pedestal in the cavity. Figure C.3 and Figure C.4 show the decoupled vs.
coupled positions for FREC-II in these configurations. The distance between the nickel plate and
the decoupled FREC-II (i.e., ≈4” at the upper grid plate, ≈2.5” at the lower plate) was estimated
from several photographs of the decoupled reactor system.

 Table C.1: Calculated neuronic effects of complete water vs. normal nickel reflection
 Model facets with standard 236-fueled element configuration, 32” pedestal, CRs at 1202 RU, SRs and TRs raised Neutronic results

 (#2) Water: Ni elements and plates replaced with water Coupled, Ni+Al plates removed Absent 3.091 ±0.013 30.289 ±0.017
 (#3) Nickel: 69 Ni elements, Ni plates replaced with Ni elements Absent Absent 0.157 ±0.003 24.187 ±0.014

Regardless of the presence of the NRS or FREC-II, water reflection increases reactivity by ≈$3.
Although not the focus of this study, the decoupled FREC-II (with FRs down) is found to have a
small, positive reactivity worth of ≈8¢. When coupled and the FRs still down, FREC-II is worth
≈20¢. Upon raising FRs, FREC-II has significant worth when the CRs are at low position. With
nickel reflection, the standalone ACRR has a neutron generation time of ≈24 µs. The decoupled
and coupled FREC-II with FRs down cause a small increase in generation time. FREC-II coupling
with FRs up significantly increases the generation time to ≈30 µs (for a 25% increase). Water
reflection for the various configurations also yields ≈30 µs generation time—pulse width would
likewise increase by ≈25%, which is generally undesirable.

C.2 Reflector Neutron Interactions
A perturbation analysis is conducted of configuration #3 from the previous section, which simplifies
the ACRR to a hypothetical layout surrounded entirely by nickel elements (i.e., no plates, no NRS,

 67

no FREC-II). The KPERT card is used to calculate the reactivity perturbation of swapping the nickel
elements for water, effectively creating configuration #4.C.2
The perturbation analysis suggests that the local change in neutron absorption is the primary
reaction driving the increase in reactivity when nickel is replaced with water. Figure C.5 shows
the perturbation results for absorption (total neutron disappearance), elastic scattering, thermal
S(α,β) scattering, and total inelastic scattering. The total reactivity change is also depicted in
Fig. C.5. The reactivity perturbations are evaluated as a function of energy using 89 groups, and
the legend displays the total, energy-integrated reactivity values.

 Energy [MeV]
Figure C.5: Reactivity perturbation as a function of neutron energy due to changing nickel reflectors elements to
 water

Neutron absorption accounts for ≈50% of the positive reactivity change of water reflection, which
is mostly due to reduced thermal neutron absorption. Most of the remaining reactivity effect is
due to water being a superior elastic scattering material for fast neutrons with energy between
30 keV and 1 MeV. The large, positive reactivity perturbation due to elastic scattering in this
energy region is counteracted by elastic reactions below 10 eV and above 1 MeV, where the elastic
perturbation is negative. Thermal scattering with water molecules is a positive contribution. The
inelastic perturbation is slightly negative because nickel is more effective than water at mitigating
fast (⪆1 MeV) ACRR neutron leakage. With pure-water reflection, fast neutron leakage is more
C.2
 The advantage of KPERT is the ability to calculate certain details (e.g., reaction energy dependence) of reactivity
 changes due to material substitutions in a single MCNP run. Disadvantages are increased runtime and memory
 usage, possibly requiring separate runs for several energy-dependent KPERT entries.

 68

 likely to be thermalized and absorbed in the water before returning to the fuel. Primary evidence
 of these effects are the predicted reactivity changes. Examination of neutron cross section data is
 also useful. Figure C.6 shows inelastic and total scattering macroscopic cross sections for nickel
 and water.

 C.2.2 Water Displacement
 The neutronic effects of the nickel elements is partially due to displacement of adjacent pool water.
 However, when the reflector layer is limited to one row, the water displacement impact is minimal
 relative to local changes in neutron absorption. (A much thicker reflector layer can produce further
 differences.) Materials with reduced thermal neutron absorption (e.g., aluminum, zirconium, and
 lead) have distinct neutronic effects compared to nickel or water reflection.
 Table C.2 summarizes the neutronic effects of alternative materials for the reflector elements.
 Reflection with nickel and water are reiterated. The alternative reflector elements have the same
 geometry and placement as the default ACRR configuration (Fig. C.1a). Reflection by aluminum,
 zirconium, and lead—which are colloquially deemed as transparentC.4 to neutrons—are analyzed to
 gauge the effects of water displacement. Despite displacing the same amount of water as the nickel
 elements, these materials yield increased reactivity over nickel due to reduced local absorption
 of thermal neutrons. Thus, the significance of water displacement alone is secondary compared
 to neutron reactions with the reflector material (again, assuming one row of reflector elements).
 More interesting is the fact that zirconium and lead actually yield increased reactivity over water
 reflection (or, more accurately, composite reflection of zirconium+water or lead+water constitute
 enhanced reflection over water for the ACRR).

 C.4
 The “transparency” of these materials to neutrons is mainly in relation to their low absorption of thermal neutrons.

 † The reflector element material is replaced with air while the surrounding water in the hexagonal unit cell remains.
 ‡ The entire hexagonal unit cell is replaced with air including the water.

To better isolate the effects of water displacement, two cases are considered that replace the
reflection layer with air. The “air” case replaces only the cylindrical extent of the reflector element
with air, leaving the surrounding water of the hexagonal unit cell. This case is representative of
surrounding the ACRR with hollow (i.e., filled with air or helium) aluminum elements, assuming
a physical means of preventing hollow elements from floating away. The “void” case (somewhat
unrealistically) replaces the entire hexagonal unit cell with air. Both these cases produce less
reactivity than nickel and have increased neutron generation time of >32 µs, providing additional
evidence that the nickel elements have other neutronic effects besides water displacement.
Aluminum, zirconium, and lead also exhibit increased neutron generation time ⪆30 µs, and these
materials perturb the core power distribution enough to reduce regulating rod worths. Thus, in
the context of reactor design modifications, replacing core materials with alternatives that appear
neutronically benign can nonetheless produce significant neutronic differences. Analyzing these
changes is essential to properly inform pulse kinetics and fuel thermal-mechanical performance.
Table C.2 also includes results for Inconel 625 and 304 stainless steel. As a reflector layer that is
one row thick, these materials are neutronically similar to pure nickel. Although excess reactivity
is slightly reduced, rods worths and neutron generation times are similar. If pure-nickel elements
were not feasible for future core configurations or new CREST designs, Inconel or stainless steel
may be reasonable alternatives.

C.3 Power Distribution and Cavity Radiation Magnitudes
Select cavity radiation metrics are compared with nickel and water reflection in Table C.3. These
metrics are evaluated with the CRs positioned for a large pulse. Percentage values in parentheses
indicate 1-σ relative errors. The flatter power distribution associated with water reflection decreases
cavity radiation magnitudes by ≈10%. Despite the increased thermal fluence (and the associated
increased importance of thermal fissions) in the outer core, the cavity spectrum remains unchanged
in the water-reflected case. Cavity neutron spectrum for the ACRR is mostly dependent on the
local geometry at the cavity-lattice interface (e.g., the water gap between the cavity and the nearest
fuel row). The fuel material and lattice pitch can also influence the cavity spectrum.

Table C.3 also lists the magnitude and location maximum power peaking. Maximum peaking
occurs in the second hexagonal fuel row for both nickel and water reflection. With nickel reflection,
maximum peaking occurs near TR-C; with water, maximum peaking occurs near TR-A. As shown
in Figure C.9, the water-reflected core power distribution is significantly flatter than the default,
nickel-reflected core. Maximum PF with water reflection is ≈10% lower (1.32 vs. 1.47 for nickel
reflection), which tracks well with the reduced (per MJ) cavity radiation observed with water
reflection.
Figure C.9 depicts a mesh tally of core-level fission heating—sub-element fission heat generation
gradients are visible on this plot. Neutron fluence distributions for nickel and water reflection
are provided in Figure C.10 (thermal fluence), Figure C.11 (fast fluence), and Figure C.13 (total
fluence). The fluence contour magnitudes have units of cm2nMJ . With water reflection, thermal
fluence at the core periphery is markedly increased. The increased thermal fluence causes greater
fission heating on the outer edges of the periphery fuel elements. Edge fission heating in the outer
core is suppressed by the nickel reflector’s absorption of thermal neutrons that would otherwise
return to the core from the pool water.
